```text
1	\documentclass[11pt,a4paper]{article}
2	\usepackage[review]{acl}
3	% \usepackage{acl}  % uncomment for camera-ready (remove [review])
4	\usepackage{times,latexsym}
5	\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb}
6	\usepackage{graphicx}
7	\graphicspath{{../../figures/paper/}}
8	\usepackage{booktabs}
9	\usepackage{multirow}
10	\usepackage{xcolor}
11	\usepackage{algorithm}
12	\usepackage{algorithmic}

14	\title{Less Is More: Why Exception Events Outperform Complete Traces \\for Verdict Prediction}
15	\author{Anonymous ACL submission}
16	
17	\begin{document}
18	\maketitle
19	
20	\begin{abstract}
21	Code language models increasingly incorporate execution traces, yet more trace information does not guarantee better performance.
22	We present the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction---classifying whether a test passes or fails---across four models spanning three architecture families and a 1.5B--13B parameter range.
23	Exception events alone achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% accuracy on three of four models, while complete traces reach only ${\sim}$80\% despite containing strictly more information.
24	Training dynamics reveal why: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 training steps, whereas even \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss (gradient dilution eliminated) oscillates throughout training without reaching stable accuracy---overfitting to spurious patterns in verbose content rather than learning the verdict signal.
25	Decomposition analysis on Qwen-7B attributes the gap to information overload and gradient dilution from low-density tokens, and a tail-truncation budget sweep confirms a sharp phase transition between compact and verbose inputs.
26	These results demonstrate that for trace-based verdict prediction, information density determines learning efficiency far more than information quantity.
27	\end{abstract}
28	
29	\input{introduction}
30	\input{method}
31	\input{experiments}
32	\input{discussion}
33	\input{related_work}
34	\input{conclusion}
35	
36	\section*{Limitations}
37	
38	Our study has several limitations.
39	First, our primary experiments use a single dataset (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); while a cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench (\S\ref{sec:debugbench}) confirms that exception dominance generalizes to synthetically injected bugs, generalization to other languages, larger codebases, or real-world fault distributions remains untested.
40	Second, while we evaluate four models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters across three architecture families, all are code-specialized; generalization to general-purpose models or larger scales remains untested.
41	Third, DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\% ($\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), which we hypothesize reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity in DeepSeek-Coder, though direct evidence is lacking (fail recall = 70.5\% for the outlier vs.\ 100\% for others).
42	Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} shows perfect seed robustness ($\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$), but Qwen \texttt{ae\_only} also exhibits seed variance ($\sigma{=}$0.40pp; seed 123 = 99.08\% vs.\ seeds 42/456 = 99.92\%), though far smaller than DeepSeek's ($\sigma{=}$4.76pp).
43	Whether training stabilization techniques can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored; other conditions use a single seed.
44	Fourth, the initial DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} run was invalidated by context overflow; a re-run with extended context yields 79.16\% (CI [76.80\%, 81.30\%]), 2.60pp below Qwen (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
45	Fifth, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one of our evaluation models; this does not affect verdict prediction but may influence the pass/fail distribution.
46	Sixth, self-familiarity bias may inflate absolute accuracy: both Qwen-2.5-Coder and DeepSeek-Coder were likely exposed to HumanEval and MBPP solutions during pretraining, so the models may partially recognize solutions rather than purely reasoning from trace signals; our cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench mitigates but does not eliminate this concern.
47	Seventh, the error distribution in our test set may not reflect real-world fault distributions: exceptions (especially \texttt{TypeError}, \texttt{ValueError}) dominate LLM-generated failures, but production code may exhibit a broader spectrum of silent logic errors where no exception is raised, potentially reducing the effectiveness of exception-based verdict prediction.
48	Finally, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; whether the information density advantage extends to other trace-based tasks is an open question.
49	
50	\section*{Ethics Statement}
51	
52	This work uses publicly available benchmarks (HumanEval, MBPP-sanitized, DebugBench) and does not involve human subjects, crowdsourced annotations, or personally identifiable information.
53	All models evaluated are open-source and publicly accessible.
54	Training uses parameter-efficient LoRA fine-tuning on two RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPUs, with each experiment using a single GPU.
55	Total compute is approximately 320 GPU-hours across all conditions, models, seeds, and iterative refinements (including approximately 170 hours for successful experiments; individual runs range from 0.5h for small models to 2.2h for CodeLlama-13B, with the total reflecting 4 models $\times$ multiple conditions $\times$ multiple seeds $\times$ iterative development).
56	We do not foresee direct negative societal impacts from this work; verdict prediction is a developer-facing tool for automated testing.
57	
58	\bibliography{references}
59	
60	\appendix
61	\input{appendix}
62	
63	\end{document}
64
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Execution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.
4	Yet feeding thousands of trace lines to a model can be no better than feeding none: the diagnostic signal drowns in routine execution steps.
5	\emph{Which trace components actually carry the signal that matters?}
6	
7	Prior work found no single trace representation consistently outperforms others \citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks.
8	\emph{Verdict prediction}---classifying whether a test passes or fails---offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, enabling controlled component ablation.
9	As fault detection, the model need not simulate execution, only determine whether something went wrong---and if that signal is concentrated in a small trace subset, information density should matter more than quantity.
10	
11	We test this hypothesis through the first systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.
12	Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program}, we decompose a multi-component trace representation (VOTA: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression) into individual components across four models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters and three architecture families: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \citep{qwen2025coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \citep{deepseek2024coder}.
13	The results are striking: complete traces (${\sim}$80\%) underperform a single compact component (${\geq}$99\%) despite containing strictly more information---a direct demonstration that information density, not quantity, determines learning efficiency.
14	
15	\textbf{Exception events alone achieve ${\geq}$99.83\% verdict accuracy on three of four models (${\geq}$99.33\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds; CodeLlama-13B seed 123 reaches 100\%).}
16	DeepSeek-6.7B is the exception: its 3-seed mean is 96.58\% ($\pm$4.76pp) due to one seed exhibiting format collapse (fail recall = 70.5\%).
17	This near-perfect accuracy holds from 1.5B to 13B parameters, while the code-only baseline (\texttt{no\_trace}) varies from 67.3\% to 76.5\% with strong scale dependence.
18	Training dynamics expose the mechanism (Figure~\ref{fig:training_dynamics}): \texttt{exception\_only} converges to 99.33\% within 50 training steps and saturates at 99.83\% by step 150, while even \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss (gradient dilution eliminated) oscillates between 82\% and 89\% across the entire training without stable convergence, consistent with overfitting to spurious patterns in verbose content.
19	
20	Adding trace information beyond exceptions provides no benefit: VOTA (${\geq}$98.57\% across all models, excluding outlier seeds) slightly underperforms \texttt{exception\_only} on Qwen-7B (99.05\% vs.\ 99.83\%), with both conditions achieving near-ceiling accuracy across architectures.
21	The performance gap approximately decomposes into information overload (${\sim}$76\%) and gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) on Qwen-7B (3 seeds).
22	A tail-truncation budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tokens, with \texttt{tail\_2048} recovering 95--98\% of \texttt{exception\_only}'s accuracy.
23	Even on silent logic errors (55.5\% of failures), \texttt{exception\_only} maintains 100\% recall because test assertions generate exception events.
24	
25	Our contributions:
26	\begin{enumerate}
27	\item \textbf{Information density determines SFT learning efficiency}: training dynamics directly show that denser trace components learn faster and more stably---\texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\%) without stable convergence. Seed variance negatively correlates with input density ($\sigma{=}$0.00 to 1.12), and SFT closes a 6--49pp zero-shot gap across all models (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
28	\item \textbf{Exception events are the minimal sufficient signal}: ${\geq}$99.83\% on three of four models, ${\geq}$99.33\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds (1.5B--13B, three families), with ${\sim}$91\% semantic rather than format-driven (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}, \S\ref{sec:nomarker}). Cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench ($N{=}426$) preserves the ranking, though margins narrow from ceiling effects (\S\ref{sec:debugbench}).
29	\item \textbf{Approximate density decomposition (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds)}: The performance gap decomposes into gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) and information overload (${\sim}$76\%), implying content selection matters more than loss engineering. A budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\sim}$500 tail tokens (\S\ref{sec:density}, \S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
30	\item \textbf{Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy}: Tier~1 signals (exceptions, assertions) generalize across three models (${\geq}$99\%), while Tier~2 (loop compression) is model-dependent: Qwen +10.9pp, DeepSeek +7.9pp, CodeLlama +0.6pp over no trace (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
31	\end{enumerate}
32	
```

Seed variance negatively correlates with input density ($\\sigma{=}$0.00 to 1.12), and SFT closes a 6--49pp zero-shot gap across all models (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\\item \\textbf{Exception events are the minimal sufficient signal}: ${\\geq}$99.83\\% on three of four models, ${\\geq}$99.33\\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds (1.5B--13B, three families), with ${\\sim}$91\\% semantic rather than format-driven (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}, \\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}). Cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench ($N{=}426$) preserves the ranking, though margins narrow from ceiling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:debugbench}).\n\\item \\textbf{Approximate density decomposition (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds)}: The performance gap decomposes into gradient dilution (${\\sim}$24\\%) and information overload (${\\sim}$76\\%), implying content selection matters more than loss engineering. A budget sweep across four models reveals a phase transition at ${\\sim}$500 tail tokens (\\S\\ref{sec:density}, \\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\\item \\textbf{Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy}: Tier~1 signals (exceptions, assertions) generalize across three models (${\\geq}$99\\%), while Tier~2 (loop compression) is model-dependent: Qwen +10.9pp, DeepSeek +7.9pp, CodeLlama +0.6pp over no trace (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	
3	\subsection{Task Definition}
4	
5	We study \emph{verdict prediction}: given a Python function, a test input, and an optional execution trace, predict whether the test passes or fails.
6	The model produces a single token (\texttt{pass} or \texttt{fail}), making this a binary classification task.
7	Unlike code generation \citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution.
8	The discrete binary outcome makes the effect of each trace component directly measurable, providing cleaner ablation contrasts than generation tasks where output quality varies continuously.
9	
10	\subsection{Dataset}
11	\label{sec:dataset}
12	
13	We sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} (164) and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} (427), executed each against canonical tests with \texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 pairs (67\% pass, 33\% fail).
14	The dataset is split 80/20 by problem ID into 4,720 training and 1,190 test samples (majority baseline: 71.51\%).
15	The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (39.45\% train, 48.99\% test duplicates); these duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem, with the train--test split enforced at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}).
16	
17	\subsection{Trace Representations}
18	
19	We define seven conditions that vary the trace information available (Figure~\ref{fig:trace_overview}):
20	\begin{itemize}
21	\item \texttt{no\_trace}: Source code and test input only.
22	\item \texttt{full\_trace}: Complete line-by-line trace with variable states.
23	\item \texttt{VOTA}: Exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression (Appendix~\ref{sec:vota_algorithm}).
24	\item \texttt{exception\_only}: Only exception/error events.
25	\item \texttt{ae\_only}: Only assertion-point actual/expected pairs.
26	\item \texttt{loop\_only}: Only loop compression summaries.
27	\item \texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae}: VOTA without assertion-point information.
28	\end{itemize}
29	
30	\begin{figure*}[t]
31	\centering
32	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_trace_overview}
33	\caption{Overview of the verdict prediction pipeline. Source code and a test input are executed to produce a full raw trace. Four conditions vary the trace information: \texttt{no\_trace} (75.13\%), \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%), VOTA (99.41\%), and \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%). Accuracies for Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$). Appendix~\ref{sec:vota_examples} provides input/output examples.}
34	\label{fig:trace_overview}
35	\end{figure*}
36	
37	\subsection{Models and Training}
38	
39	We evaluate four models spanning three architecture families: \textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct} \citep{qwen2025coder}, \textbf{CodeLlama-13B-Instruct} \citep{roziere2024code}, and \textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \citep{deepseek2024coder}, covering a 1.5B--13B parameter range (8.7$\times$).
40	All conditions use LoRA \citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) on all linear layers, 3 epochs, effective batch size 32, LR $2 \times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup/decay, max sequence length 2{,}048 tokens.
41	Sequences exceeding this limit are truncated while preserving the verdict label.
42	The primary seed is 42; seeds 123 and 456 test \texttt{exception\_only} robustness (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_details}).
43	
44	\subsection{Loss Ablation}
45	
46	To disentangle training signal from input signal quality, we introduce \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).
47	Improvement over standard \texttt{full\_trace} is attributable to \emph{gradient dilution}; the remaining gap to VOTA reflects \emph{information overload} at inference time.
48	
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate across two research questions:
4	(1)~Do Tier~1 exception signals generalize across models?
5	(2)~What is the relative contribution of each trace component?
6	All experiments use $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\% CIs.\footnote{Data is partitioned at the problem level (472 train / 119 test problems, zero crossover; \S\ref{sec:dataset}). The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses passing solutions into identical rows (48.99\% test duplicates; Appendix~\ref{sec:dedup_stats}), but these arise from within-problem variants, not cross-set leakage; deduplication sensitivity analysis confirms 0.00pp delta across all 19 experimental conditions.}
7	Pairwise comparisons use paired bootstrap resampling ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42); all differences are significant at $p{<}0.0001$ unless noted.
8	
9	\subsection{Cross-Model Comparison}
10	\label{sec:cross_model}
11	
12	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} compares four models spanning an 8.7$\times$ parameter range (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families (Qwen, DeepSeek, CodeLlama) under key conditions.
13	
14	\begin{table}[t]
15	\centering
16	\caption{Cross-model verdict accuracy (\%, $N{=}1{,}190$) across four models (1.5B--13B) and three architecture families. Multi-seed entries report mean$\pm$std over 3 seeds. Bold: best per model. Full per-seed breakdowns in Appendix~\ref{sec:full_results}; per-class metrics in Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}.}
17	\label{tab:cross_model}
18	\small
19	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
20	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
21	\toprule
22	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Qwen-7B} & \textbf{Qwen-1.5B} & \textbf{CL-13B} & \textbf{DS-6.7B} \\
23	\midrule
24	exception\_only & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.83}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.00} & \textbf{99.89}{\scriptsize$\pm$0.10} & \textbf{99.33}$^\dag$ \\
25	ae\_only & 99.64{\scriptsize$\pm$0.49} & --- & --- & 98.74 \\
26	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & 99.58 & 98.57 & 99.25$^\ddag$ \\
27	loop\_only & 86.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.52} & --- & 77.06 & 81.26 \\
28	no\_trace & 75.13 & 67.31 & 76.47 & 73.36 \\
29	\midrule
30	Majority baseline & \multicolumn{4}{c}{71.51} \\
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}%
33	}
34	\vspace{2pt}
35	\par\raggedright\footnotesize
36	$^\dag$DeepSeek 3-seed mean 96.58\%$\pm$4.76pp; seed 123 (91.09\%, fail recall\,=\,70.5\%) exhibits format collapse on long inputs (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}), reported as 2-seed mean excluding the outlier.
37	VOTA on Qwen-7B: 99.41/98.66/99.08 ($\sigma{=}$0.38).
38	\texttt{ae\_only} 3 seeds: 99.92/99.08/99.92 (mean 99.64\%); Qwen-7B \texttt{loop\_only} 3 seeds: 85.63/85.88/86.64 (mean 86.05\%).
39	Qwen-1.5B \texttt{exception\_only}: 99.83\% across 3 seeds ($\sigma{=}$0).
40	$^\ddag$DeepSeek VOTA 3 seeds: 84.45/99.16/99.33; seed 42 (84.45\%, fail recall\,=\,45.4\%) is an outlier due to tokenizer-driven truncation (Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}), reported as 2-seed mean.
41	\end{table}
42	
43	\paragraph{Exception dominance across families.}
44	Under \texttt{exception\_only}, the Qwen and CodeLlama families achieve ${\geq}$99.83\% (mean): Qwen-7B (99.83\%$\pm$0.00, 3 seeds), Qwen-1.5B (99.83\%), and CodeLlama-13B (99.89\%$\pm$0.10, 3 seeds; seed 123 achieves 100\%).
45	DeepSeek-6.7B reaches 99.33\% on 2/3 seeds with one LoRA-sensitivity outlier at 91.09\% (seed 123; fail recall = 70.5\%).
46	Excluding the DeepSeek outlier, the cross-model range is 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\%), the tightest of any condition.
47	
48	\paragraph{Scale invariance of exception signals.}
49	Exception-based accuracy is shared by models spanning 1.5B to 13B parameters (8.7$\times$): both Qwen-1.5B and CodeLlama-13B achieve 99.83\%+ on the same representation.
50	CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\% (0/1190 errors), confirming that Qwen's two persistent errors (from \texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\ref{sec:irreducible}) are high-probability convergence artifacts rather than hard data limitations.
51	In contrast, \texttt{no\_trace} scales monotonically with capacity: 67.31\% (1.5B) $\to$ 75.13\% (7B) $\to$ 76.47\% (13B), a 9.16pp range that exception events completely eliminate (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_scale}).
52	
53	\begin{figure}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}
56	\caption{Verdict accuracy vs.\ model scale. \texttt{exception\_only} maintains ${\geq}$99.33\% regardless of scale (1.5B--13B), while \texttt{no\_trace} shows monotonic scale sensitivity (+9.16pp from 1.5B to 13B). The \texttt{nomarker} condition (format markers removed) shows no degradation at smaller scale (${\sim}$97.7--97.8\%).}
57	\label{fig:cross_scale}
58	\end{figure}
59	
60	\paragraph{VOTA cross-family variation.}
61	Excluding outlier seeds, VOTA achieves ${\geq}$98.57\% across all four models: 99.58\% (Qwen-1.5B) $>$ 99.25\%$\pm$0.12 (DeepSeek-6.7B, 2 seeds) $>$ 99.05\%$\pm$0.38 (Qwen-7B, 3 seeds) $>$ 98.57\% (CodeLlama-13B; CI [97.9, 99.2]), a 1.01pp range.
62	DeepSeek seed 42 (84.45\%, fail recall = 45.4\%) is an outlier driven by tokenizer-induced truncation (91.7\% of training samples affected; Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}); the remaining seeds (99.16\%, 99.33\%) match cross-model performance.
63	\texttt{exception\_only} inputs are compact (median 150 tokens), sidestepping truncation entirely (Appendix~\ref{sec:robustness_analysis}).
64	
65	\subsection{Component Ablation}
66	\label{sec:ablation}
67	
68	Multi-seed \texttt{ae\_only} ablations use Qwen-7B; \texttt{loop\_only} is evaluated across three models to test Tier~2 generalization (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
69	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} decomposes VOTA on Qwen-2.5-7B and reveals a two-tier hierarchy: Tier~1 (\texttt{exception\_only}) generalizes across all four models (${\geq}$99.33\%), while Tier~2 (\texttt{loop\_only}) is model-dependent.
70	
71	\begin{table}[t]
72	\centering
73	\caption{Component ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$). Tier~1 signals achieve near-perfect accuracy; combining them (VOTA) slightly dilutes performance. $^\dag$\texttt{ae\_only}: 3-seed mean 99.64\%$\pm$0.49 (seeds 42/123/456: 99.92/99.08/99.92). \texttt{exception\_only}: 99.83\%$\pm$0.00 (3 seeds). $^\S$VOTA: 3-seed mean 99.05\%$\pm$0.38 (99.41/98.66/99.08). $^\ddag$\texttt{loop\_only}: 3-seed mean 86.05\%$\pm$0.52 (85.63/85.88/86.64). Seed variance negatively correlates with information density: $\sigma{=}$0.00 (exception) ${<}$ 0.38 (VOTA) ${<}$ 0.49 (ae) ${<}$ 0.52 (loop).}
74	\label{tab:ablation}
75	\small
76	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
77	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
78	\toprule
79	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Components} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{Tier} \\
80	\midrule
81	ae\_only & assertion-point & 99.64$^\dag$ & [99.26, 99.87] & 1 \\
82	exception\_only & exception events & \textbf{99.83} & [99.58, 100.00] & 1 \\
83	VOTA & exc+ae+loop & 99.05$^\S$ & [98.49, 99.50] & --- \\
84	VOTA\_no\_ae & exc+loop & 99.41 & [98.90, 99.80] & --- \\
85	loop\_only & loop compression & 86.05$^\ddag$ & [83.97, 87.96] & 2 \\
86	full\_trace & complete trace & 81.76 & [79.70, 83.90] & --- \\
87	no\_trace & none & 75.13 & [72.77, 77.48] & --- \\
88	\midrule
89	Regex (best) & exc.\ type regex & 93.61 & --- & --- \\
90	3-shot (Qwen-7B) & in-context learning & 90.84 & --- & --- \\
91	Zero-shot (4 models) & prompting & 51.0--93.5 & --- & --- \\
92	\bottomrule
93	\end{tabular}%
94	}
95	\end{table}
96	
97	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%$\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\%$\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}0.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.
98	Removing assertion points has no effect (\texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae} = 99.41\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.
99	Tier~2 \texttt{loop\_only} lifts over \texttt{no\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen-7B +10.92pp (86.05\%, $p{<}0.0001$), DeepSeek +7.90pp (81.26\%, CI [79.08, 83.45]), CodeLlama +0.59pp (77.06\%, n.s.).
100	\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}
101	The strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}0.001$).
102	The gap concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases: regex flags all exception events as failures, while SFT learns to distinguish caught exceptions (benign, inside \texttt{try/except}) from uncaught faults, correctly resolving 6/8 boundary samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
103	Across trace formats, SFT consistently outperforms the best applicable regex, from +3.62pp (\texttt{no\_trace} over majority baseline) to +23.86pp (VOTA), with the largest gains on formats requiring semantic interpretation (Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
104	
105	\paragraph{Zero-shot and few-shot LLM baselines.}
106	Zero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\% across four models (42.44pp range), with performance uncorrelated with model size (Qwen-1.5B: 93.45\% vs.\ CodeLlama-13B: 51.01\%).
107	Three-shot ICL on Qwen-7B (90.84\%) improves only 1.76pp and still trails SFT by 8.99pp.
108	SFT compresses this 42.44pp range to 0.56pp (99.33--99.89\%), closing a 6--49pp gap with 4,720 training samples (Appendix~\ref{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}).
109	
110	\subsection{Format Marker Ablation}
111	\label{sec:nomarker}
112	
113	The \texttt{exception\_only} format includes a \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker in all pass-class samples (regex baseline B5 exploits this for 99.33\% accuracy; Appendix~\ref{sec:regex_baselines}).
114	To quantify the marker's contribution, we retrain with all format markers removed (\texttt{nomarker} condition).
115	
116	Qwen-7B 3-seed mean: 97.65\% $\pm$ 0.96 (seeds 42/123/456: 96.55/98.32/98.07\%), a drop of 2.18pp from \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%).
117	Qwen-1.5B: 97.82\%, drop of 2.01pp.
118	The marker contribution (${\sim}$2pp) accounts for only ${\sim}$9\% of the total exception lift over \texttt{no\_trace} (24.70pp on 7B).
119	Exception events thus provide two signal layers that show no degradation at smaller scale: structural markers (${\sim}$2pp, regex-learnable) and semantic exception content (${\sim}$22pp, requiring SFT to distinguish caught from uncaught exceptions).
120	
121	\subsection{Information Density Decomposition}
122	\label{sec:density}
123	
124	The 17.29pp gap between \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%) and VOTA (99.05\%, 3-seed mean) decomposes via \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only} (85.94\%$\pm$1.12, 3 seeds; Table~\ref{tab:density}), which retains the full trace but masks all trace tokens from the loss.
125	
126	\begin{table}[t]
127	\centering
128	\caption{Approximate information density decomposition: gradient dilution (${\sim}$24\%) vs.\ information overload (${\sim}$76\%). \texttt{label\_only} and VOTA are 3-seed means on Qwen-7B.}
129	\label{tab:density}
130	\small
131	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
132	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
133	\toprule
134	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{$\Delta$ vs.\ full\_trace} & \textbf{Gap explained} \\
135	\midrule
136	full\_trace & 81.76 & --- & --- \\
137	label\_only & 85.94{\scriptsize$\pm$1.12} & +4.18pp & 24\% \\
138	VOTA & 99.05{\scriptsize$\pm$0.38} & +17.29pp & 100\% \\
139	\bottomrule
140	\end{tabular}%
141	}
142	\end{table}
143	
144	Gradient dilution accounts for approximately 24\% of the gap (+4.18pp, 3-seed mean): when the model no longer wastes gradient updates on predicting trace tokens, accuracy improves even with identical input.
145	The remaining ${\sim}$76\% (+13.11pp) reflects information overload at inference: the model receives thousands of irrelevant trace lines that compete for attention with the diagnostic signal, regardless of loss design.
146	This asymmetry implies that trace compression methods should prioritize \emph{content selection} (removing irrelevant lines) over \emph{loss engineering} (masking non-target tokens), as the larger bottleneck is what the model sees, not how it is trained.
147	The two factors may interact, so this decomposition is approximate.
148	Label-only loss simultaneously changes multiple optimization dynamics (effective gradient norm, implicit regularization from trace token prediction); the 24\%/76\% attribution should be interpreted as an approximate decomposition rather than a strict causal partition.
149	This analysis uses 3 seeds on a single model (Qwen-7B); cross-model validation remains future work.
150	
151	\subsection{Additional Analyses}
152	\label{sec:additional}
153	
154	\paragraph{Trace length ablation.}
155	\label{sec:trace_length}
156	Varying the head-truncated trace token budget (256--no limit) on Qwen-7B yields accuracy in a narrow 77.2\%--81.8\% band with a non-monotonic pattern: 1024-token prefixes (77.23\%) underperform 512-token prefixes (80.42\%, $p{=}0.002$), because mid-length prefixes retain enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events near the trace terminus (Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}).
157	This non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation; under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for two of three models (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} in Appendix), with only DeepSeek showing a mild architecture-specific reversal at tail-1024$\to$tail-2048 ($-$1.5pp).
158	
159	\paragraph{Truncation direction and budget curve.}
160	Tail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 exceeds head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp, with the gap widening at smaller scale (Qwen-1.5B: +22.6pp vs.\ Qwen-7B: +15.5pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:trace_length_full}, Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}).
161	All four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp), indicating that signal-poor conditions produce a shared performance floor regardless of scale or architecture.
162	
163	A cross-model budget sweep across four models and three architecture families reveals a phase transition localized between 384 and 512 tail tokens.
164	At tail-384, all models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\%, only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256); the steep jump occurs at 384$\to$512 (+11.0--22.0pp), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling (${\sim}$94--98\%).
165	This localizes the critical threshold to ${\sim}$400--500 tokens, matching the typical exception traceback length (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}).
166	Beyond 512 tokens, accuracy converges further: multi-seed validation across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations confirms all tail-512 accuracies fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\%, 98.24\%] (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}); at tail-1024, three models reach 96.5--98.2\%, compressing the 6.3pp cross-model spread observed at tail-256.
167	\texttt{tail\_2048} captures 95--98\% of \texttt{exception\_only}'s accuracy across all completed configurations.
168	The residual 2--5pp gap confirms that semantic extraction (identifying exception type and message) still outperforms positional extraction (retaining a fixed window containing non-diagnostic lines).
169	
170	\paragraph{Calibration.}
171	\label{sec:calibration}
172	Calibration mirrors the tier hierarchy: \texttt{exception\_only} achieves ECE = 0.0027 vs.\ 0.171 for \texttt{no\_trace}, a 63$\times$ gap (Figure~\ref{fig:calibration}).
173	This calibration advantage generalizes across architectures and scales: all four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 under \texttt{exception\_only} (Appendix~\ref{sec:calibration_full}).
174	
175	\paragraph{Error orthogonality.}
176	\texttt{exception\_only}'s 2 errors have zero overlap with all other conditions' error sets (Jaccard = 0.000), confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension (Appendix~\ref{sec:error_overlap}).
177	
178	\paragraph{Exception type breakdown.}
179	A per-exception-type breakdown (Appendix~\ref{sec:exception_type}) confirms that all standard exception types achieve 100\% accuracy; the only failures arise from \texttt{GeneratorExit}, a benign \texttt{BaseException} subclass.
180	
181	\paragraph{Cross-dataset generalization.}
182	\label{sec:debugbench}
183	On DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench} ($N{=}426$), the ranking is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} 100\%, VOTA 99.77\%, \texttt{no\_trace} 96.71\%; narrow margins reflect a ceiling effect from structurally distinctive mutations (Appendix~\ref{sec:debugbench_full}).
184	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	
3	The central finding is that information density determines learning efficiency for trace-based verdict prediction.
4	Exception events concentrate the runtime's primary fault signal---error type, stack frame, and message---in a compact format (median 150 tokens), while non-exception components (variable states, loop iterations, control flow) record \emph{how} the program ran, not \emph{whether} it failed.
5	
6	This density perspective explains three key results: (1)~adding trace information beyond exceptions provides no benefit (VOTA $<$ \texttt{exception\_only}, Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), because low-density content competes for attention; (2)~a budget sweep reveals a phase transition at 384$\to$512 tail tokens (+11.0--22.0pp), matching the boundary where diagnostic events dominate; (3)~the 6.22pp SFT advantage over regex concentrates on caught-exception boundary cases, confirming semantic signal extraction.
7	The 0.56pp cross-model gap supports that signal density, not model capacity, is the binding constraint.
8	
9	\paragraph{Why exception\_only outperforms ae\_only.}
10	Although \texttt{ae\_only} is a strict superset, it achieves only 99.64\%$\pm$0.49 vs.\ \texttt{exception\_only}'s 99.83\%$\pm$0.00, indicating extra content introduces noise rather than signal.
11	
12	\paragraph{SFT vs.\ regex: semantic understanding.}
13	A regex baseline (B5) achieves 99.33\% by exploiting a format marker, but a \emph{nomarker} ablation (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}) resolves this concern: removing markers drops accuracy by only ${\sim}$2pp (Qwen-7B 97.65\%, Qwen-1.5B 97.82\%), leaving a 4.04pp gap over the best non-trivial regex (93.61\%).
14	On 8 boundary samples where pass-labeled code triggers exceptions, SFT classifies 6/8 correctly vs.\ 0/8 for regex (McNemar $p{=}0.041$), confirming ${\sim}$91\% of the signal is semantic.
15	
16	\paragraph{Why zero-shot and few-shot prompting fall short.}
17	Zero-shot prompting spans 51.0--93.5\% (42.44pp range), which SFT compresses to 0.56pp; three-shot ICL (90.84\%) improves marginally, confirming the bottleneck is verdict calibration rather than exception recognition.
18	
19	\paragraph{Near-perfect floor, not hard ceiling.}
20	The 2 errors (both \texttt{GeneratorExit}; Appendix~\ref{sec:error_overlap}) are shared across configurations; CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\%, confirming 99.83\% is a convergence \emph{floor}, signal-limited rather than capacity-limited.
21	
22	\paragraph{Tokenizer efficiency.}
23	CodeLlama's tokenizer produces ${\sim}$20\% more tokens than Qwen's on identical inputs; a label-only loss variant recovers accuracy at tail-1024 but collapses at tail-2048, indicating LoRA long-sequence instability (Appendix~\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).
24	
25	\paragraph{Tier hierarchy and methodological implications.}
26	The two-tier structure (Tier~1 ${\geq}$99\% across models; Tier~2 = 77.1--86.1\%) suggests a fallback design: extract exception events first (median 150 tokens), resort to Tier~2 only when no exception is raised (model-dependent, +0.6--10.9pp over no-trace).
27	The methodological insight---that information density determines learning efficiency---transfers to any trace-based task where compact diagnostic events exist within verbose traces, such as re-analyzing archived CI/CD logs when the original test environment is unavailable.
28	
29	\paragraph{Density and cross-model robustness.}
30	Tier~1 and Tier~2 exhibit contrasting cross-model stability: \texttt{exception\_only} spans 0.56pp (${\geq}$99.33\%), while \texttt{loop\_only} spans 8.99pp (77.06--86.05\%), with lifts following +10.9 $>$ +7.9 $>$ +0.6pp (the last not significant).
31	However, $N{=}3$ models is insufficient to establish a continuous relationship, and the correlation is confounded: Tier~1 signals are both high-density \emph{and} direct fault indicators, so density cannot be disentangled from signal type.
32	This is further corroborated by a negative result: applying label-only loss to DeepSeek full traces---where 91.7\% of samples are truncated---causes complete model collapse (28.5\% accuracy, eval loss diverges to NaN), confirming that information density in the trace content, not the loss function, is the binding constraint on learning.
33	
34	\paragraph{Exception events capture test verdict signals, not just crashes.}
35	Although 55.5\% of failures are silent logic errors, \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 100\% fail recall because test assertions generate \texttt{AssertionError} events, confirming ``exception events'' encompasses both crashes and assertion failures.
36	
37	\begin{figure}[t]
38	\centering
39	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}
40	\caption{Training dynamics comparison. Exception-only (blue) converges to 99.33\% within 50 steps and saturates at 99.83\% by step 150. Full-trace with label-only loss (red, dashed) oscillates between 82\% and 89\% across all 444 training steps without stable convergence, consistent with overfitting to spurious patterns in verbose trace content.}
41	\label{fig:training_dynamics}
42	\end{figure}
43	
44	\paragraph{Information density and learning dynamics.}
45	\texttt{exception\_only} reaches 99.33\% at step 50 and saturates at 99.83\% by step 150 (Figure~\ref{fig:training_dynamics}), while \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates between 82\% and 89\% throughout, with train loss reaching 0.0085 while eval stalls at 88\%, confirming overfitting (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
46	Seed variance negatively correlates with density ($\sigma{=}$0.00/0.38/1.12 for exception/VOTA/label-only), confirming compact inputs stabilize learning.
47	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We presented the first systematic component ablation for verdict prediction across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), finding that information density determines learning efficiency: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} (label-only loss) oscillates without stable convergence.
4	Exception events achieve ${\geq}$99.83\% on three of four models with consistent cross-model generalization, while lower-density loop signals are model-dependent (+0.6--10.9pp); generalization beyond LLM-generated Python code remains open.
5	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}
4	TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples; \citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility task- and format-dependent, which we extend by identifying exception events as the dominant signal.
5	NExT \citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} used trace chain-of-thought for repair and execution reasoning; \citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, supporting our density finding.
6	\citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained on execution traces; CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, extended to 19 languages \citep{xu2025cruxevalx}, established execution reasoning benchmarks.
7	None decompose which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy.
8	
9	\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}
10	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; DeepDFA \citep{steenhoek2024deepdfa} and learned program slicing \citep{yadavally2024slicing} combined classical analysis with deep learning.
11	In patch correctness, \citet{tian2020patchcorrectness}, CC2Vec \citep{hoang2020cc2vec}, LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch} (84.4\%), and APPT \citep{appt2024} (79.7\%) developed representation learning of code changes.
12	Verdict prediction is an instance of the longstanding test oracle problem \citep{barr2015oracle}; FuseFL \citep{widyasari2024fusefl} and \citet{foster2025mutation} demonstrated structured fault signals; the equivalent-mutant problem \citep{papadakis2019mutation} poses an analogous classification challenge.
13	None isolate individual trace components or compare against rule-based baselines.
14	
15	\paragraph{Fault Localization.}
16	Spectrum-based fault localization (SBFL) ranks program elements by suspiciousness from test spectra \citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar}; recent LLM-based approaches \citep{kang2024autofl,qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal, with verdict prediction serving as a prerequisite to FL.
17	
18	\paragraph{Trace Compression and Selection.}
19	TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} use selective trace extraction; RepairAgent \citep{bouzenia2025repairagent} and Agentless \citep{xia2024agentless} incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows, as do SWE-agent \citep{yang2024sweagent} and OpenHands \citep{wang2025openhands}, which close the loop between code generation, execution, and iterative revision.
20	In the prompt compression literature, LLMLingua \citep{jiang2023llmlingua} and Selective Context \citep{li2023selectivecontext} showed that pruning low-information tokens preserves downstream accuracy, corroborating our finding that compact trace formats outperform verbose ones; \citet{haque2025traces} found raw traces provide limited repair benefit.
21	Our approximate decomposition (gradient dilution ${\sim}$24\% vs.\ information overload ${\sim}$76\%) provides the first quantitative framework for \emph{why} compression helps, complementing these methods' focus on \emph{how}.
22	
23	\paragraph{Calibration and Defect Prediction.}
24	Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration,spiess2025calibration}, studies confidence-correctness alignment; our ECE ${<}$ 0.007 suggests compact inputs improve calibration. Just-in-time defect prediction \citep{kamei2013large,hoang2019deepjit,pornprasit2021jitline} shares our fault detection motivation but operates on code change features.
25	
26	\paragraph{Training Efficiency and Data Selection.}
27	Curriculum learning \citep{bengio2009curriculum}, dataset cartography via training dynamics \citep{swayamdipta2020cartography}, and data selection methods \citep{sachdeva2024dataefficient,xie2023doremi} show that data quality and ordering improve convergence, consistent with our finding that compact exceptions enable rapid convergence while verbose traces trap the model in oscillation.
28	
```

```text
1	\section{Per-Class Performance Breakdown}
2	\label{sec:per_class}
3	
4	Table~\ref{tab:per_class} reports per-class precision, recall, and F1 for all experimental conditions.
5

8	\caption{Per-class precision, recall, and F1 for all experimental conditions (seed 42). DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 is an outlier (84.45\%, fail recall = 45.4\%) due to tokenizer-driven truncation; seeds 123/456 achieve 99.16\%/99.33\%. DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} seed 42 achieves 100\% fail recall, but seed 123 drops to 70.5\% (see Table~\ref{tab:full_deepseek}).}
9	\label{tab:per_class}
10	\small

16	Full Trace        & Qwen-2.5-7B & 81.76 & 87.74 & 86.60 & 87.17 & 67.43 & 69.62 & 68.51 \\
17	Full Trace (LO)   & Qwen-2.5-7B & 85.94 & 88.21 & 93.18 & 90.63 & 80.07 & 68.73 & 73.97 \\

102	%% ============================================================
103	\section{VOTA Extraction Algorithm}
104	\label{sec:vota_algorithm}
105	
106	Algorithm~\ref{alg:vota} describes the three-component VOTA extraction pipeline applied to a raw \texttt{sys.settrace} execution log.
107	
108	\begin{algorithm}[t]
109	\caption{VOTA Trace Extraction}
110	\label{alg:vota}
111	\small
112	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
113	\REQUIRE Raw trace $T$ from \texttt{sys.settrace} (list of events)
114	\ENSURE Compressed VOTA representation $V$
115	\STATE $V \leftarrow$ empty string
116	\STATE \textbf{// Component 1: Exception summary}
117	\FOR{each event $e$ in $T$ with type \texttt{exception}}
118	  \STATE Parse traceback $\rightarrow$ extract error type, message, file, line
119	  \STATE Append \texttt{[ERROR] type: message (file:line)} to $V$
120	\ENDFOR
121	\IF{no exception events found}
122	  \STATE Append \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} to $V$
123	\ENDIF
124	\STATE \textbf{// Component 2: Assertion-point comparison}
125	\FOR{each \texttt{assert} or \texttt{return} event $e$ in $T$}
126	  \STATE Extract actual value from execution state
127	  \STATE Extract expected value from test assertion
128	  \STATE Append \texttt{[ASSERT] actual=... expected=... match=T/F} to $V$
129	\ENDFOR
130	\STATE \textbf{// Component 3: Loop compression}
131	\FOR{each consecutive repeated line sequence $S$ in $T$}
132	  \IF{$|S| \geq 2$ iterations of same loop body}
133	    \STATE Replace $S$ with single iteration + \texttt{[repeated $\times$N]}
134	  \ENDIF
135	\ENDFOR
136	\STATE Append compressed non-exception lines to $V$
137	\RETURN $V$
138	\end{algorithmic}
139	\end{algorithm}
140	
141	%% ============================================================
142	\section{VOTA Trace Examples}
143	\label{sec:vota_examples}
144	
145	We provide two representative VOTA trace examples illustrating the fail and pass cases.
146	In the fail case, the exception event provides an unambiguous fault signal.
147	In the pass case, the absence of an exception event combined with a matching assertion point confirms correct execution.
148	
149	\paragraph{Fail example.}
150	A \texttt{ZeroDivisionError} triggers an \texttt{[ERROR]} event, providing a direct fault signal that is sufficient for the model to predict \texttt{fail}.
151	
152	\begin{scriptsize}
153	\begin{verbatim}
154	## Function
155	def divide(a, b):
156	    return a / b
157	
158	## Test Input
159	divide(1, 0)
160	
161	## Expected Output
162	inf
163	
164	## VOTA Trace
165	[ERROR] ZeroDivisionError: division by zero
166	
167	## Verdict
168	fail
169	\end{verbatim}
170	\end{scriptsize}
171	
172	\paragraph{Pass example.}
173	No exception is raised, and the assertion point confirms that the actual output matches the expected value.
174	
175	\begin{scriptsize}
176	\begin{verbatim}
177	## Function
178	def add(a, b):
179	    return a + b
180	
181	## Test Input
182	add(2, 3)
183	
184	## Expected Output
185	5
186	
187	## VOTA Trace
188	[LOOP] No loops detected.
189	[ASSERT] actual=5, expected=5
190	
191	## Verdict
192	pass
193	\end{verbatim}
194	\end{scriptsize}
195	
196	These examples illustrate the core asymmetry exploited by the \texttt{exception\_only} condition: fail cases produce an explicit \texttt{[ERROR]} event, while pass cases are characterized by its absence.
197	This binary signal (presence vs.\ absence of an exception) is sufficient for near-perfect verdict prediction.
198

252	%% ============================================================
253	\section{Tokenizer and Truncation Analysis}
254	\label{sec:tokenizer_analysis}
255	
256	DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 (84.45\%) is an outlier driven by tokenizer-induced truncation.
257	The DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.
258	The training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates trace content, so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the majority of samples.
259	Seeds 123 and 456 (99.16\% and 99.33\%) demonstrate that DeepSeek can learn VOTA when LoRA initialization avoids the truncation-sensitive basin; the 2-seed mean (99.25\%$\pm$0.12) matches the cross-model VOTA range (98.57--99.58\%).
260	
261	A post-hoc group analysis on seed 42 provides directional evidence: partitioning test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\% (95\% CI [80.75\%, 85.24\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}0.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\%).
262	Per-class analysis (Table~\ref{tab:per_class}) confirms that seed 42 achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}.
263	
264	Seed robustness diverges across models: Qwen \texttt{exception\_only} produces identical 99.83\% across all three seeds ($\sigma{=}0$), while DeepSeek shows bimodal behavior: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 99.33\% (fail recall = 100\%), seed 123 yields 91.09\% (fail recall = 70.5\%).
265	Because \texttt{exception\_only} test inputs contain 48.99\% duplicates (607 unique of 1,190; \S\ref{sec:dataset}), CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials; under deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's accuracy is 99.67\% (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]).
266	
267	%% ============================================================
268	\section{Irreducible Sample Analysis}
269	\label{sec:irreducible}
270	
271	The \texttt{exception\_only} condition with Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B misclassifies exactly 2 out of 1,190 test samples across all three seeds (42, 123, 456), with 100\% overlap across seeds.
272	Both samples (IDs 901 and 905) share the following characteristics:
273	
274	\begin{itemize}
275	\item \textbf{Function:} \texttt{check\_dict\_case} (HumanEval/95), a function that checks whether all dictionary keys are uniformly upper-case or lower-case strings.
276	\item \textbf{Ground-truth label:} \texttt{pass}.
277	\item \textbf{Predicted label:} \texttt{fail} (false negative).
278	\item \textbf{Cross-seed consistency:} Both samples are misclassified identically by all three seeds, indicating a systematic rather than stochastic error.
279	\end{itemize}
280	
281	Manual inspection reveals the root cause: \texttt{check\_dict\_case} uses \texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \texttt{True} value, raising \texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.
282	Because \texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines, even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.
283	The model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \texttt{fail}.
284	This is not a model failure but a representation ambiguity: the \texttt{exception\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.
285	
286	Most configurations converge to these same 2 errors: Qwen-1.5B, Qwen-7B (3 seeds), CodeLlama-13B (seeds 42/456), and DeepSeek-6.7B (seeds 42/456) all produce error set $\{901, 905\}$.
287	However, CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\% accuracy (0/1190 errors), correctly classifying both samples.
288	This demonstrates that 99.83\% is a near-perfect \emph{floor} (the convergence attractor for most LoRA initializations) rather than a hard data ceiling: the representation supports perfect accuracy, but most initializations converge to the same 2-error basin.
289	The practical implication is that these errors are high-probability convergence artifacts addressable through seed selection or ensemble methods, not fundamental limitations of the exception-based representation.
290	
291	%% ============================================================
292	\section{Exception Type Accuracy Breakdown}
293	\label{sec:exception_type}
294	
295	Table~\ref{tab:exception_type} reports \texttt{exception\_only} accuracy stratified by exception type on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).
296	All standard exception types (\texttt{TypeError}, \texttt{ValueError}, \texttt{IndexError}, etc.) achieve 100\% accuracy.
297	The only misclassifications arise from \texttt{GeneratorExit}, a \texttt{BaseException} subclass that is not an \texttt{Exception}. It is raised by Python's iterator protocol during generator short-circuiting, not by actual program errors.

330	To test whether the \texttt{full\_trace} performance gap is caused by input truncation at training time, we vary the maximum trace token budget on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42) while keeping all other hyperparameters fixed.
331

All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump +13.7--22.0pp at 384$\to$512, converging into a 93.6--98.2\% band by tail-1024. The shaded region marks the transition zone where the exception traceback (${\sim}$400--500 tokens) is captured. Dashed line: \texttt{exception\_only} range (99.3--99.9\%); dotted line: \texttt{no\_trace} range (67--77\%).}
358	\label{fig:trace_length}
359	\end{figure}
360	
361	As shown in Table~\ref{tab:trace_length} and Figure~\ref{fig:trace_length}, all four trace length conditions fall within a 5pp range (77.2\%--81.8\%).
362	To quantify the reliability of the observed differences, Table~\ref{tab:trace_length_pairwise} reports paired bootstrap comparisons ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42) across all condition pairs.
363	
364	\begin{table}[t]
365	\centering
366	\caption{Pairwise paired bootstrap comparisons for trace length ablation ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42). $\Delta$ = accuracy difference between the two conditions (first $-$ second in the budget group; condition $-$ baseline in the other groups). **$p{<}0.01$; ***$p{<}0.001$.}

368	\small
369	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
370	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
371	\toprule
372	& \textbf{Comparison} & \textbf{$\Delta$ (pp)} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$p$-value} \\
373	\midrule
374	\multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox{90}{\scriptsize Budget}}
375	& 512 vs.\ 256           & $+$1.60  & [$-$0.34, $+$3.53]   & 0.117 \\
376	& 1024 vs.\ 512          & $-$3.19  & [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]   & 0.002** \\
377	& no\_limit vs.\ 1024    & $+$4.54  & [$+$2.52, $+$6.55]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\
378	& no\_limit vs.\ 256     & $+$2.94  & [$+$0.84, $+$5.04]   & 0.006** \\
379	\midrule
380	\multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox{90}{\scriptsize vs.\ no\_trace}}
381	& 256 $-$ no\_trace       & $+$3.70  & [$+$1.43, $+$6.05]   & 0.002** \\
382	& 512 $-$ no\_trace       & $+$5.29  & [$+$3.03, $+$7.56]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\
383	& 1024 $-$ no\_trace      & $+$2.10  & [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]   & 0.078 \\
384	& no\_limit $-$ no\_trace & $+$6.64  & [$+$4.37, $+$8.99]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\
385	\midrule
386	\multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox{90}{\scriptsize vs.\ exc.}}
387	& 256 $-$ exc\_only       & $-$21.01 & [$-$23.45, $-$18.74] & ${<}$0.001*** \\
388	& 512 $-$ exc\_only       & $-$19.41 & [$-$21.76, $-$17.14] & ${<}$0.001*** \\
389	& 1024 $-$ exc\_only      & $-$22.61 & [$-$25.04, $-$20.25] & ${<}$0.001*** \\
390	& no\_limit $-$ exc\_only & $-$18.07 & [$-$20.34, $-$15.88] & ${<}$0.001*** \\
391	\bottomrule
392	\end{tabular}%
393	}
394	\end{table}
395	
396	\paragraph{Non-monotonic pattern (head-truncation).}
397	Under head-truncation (retaining trace prefixes), the trend is non-monotonic: 512 tokens (80.42\%) slightly outperforms 256 tokens (78.82\%), but doubling to 1024 tokens \emph{decreases} accuracy to 77.23\%, a statistically significant 3.19pp drop ($p{=}0.002$, 95\% CI [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]).
398	The no-limit condition (81.76\%) recovers and significantly exceeds 1024 tokens ($p{<}0.001$), but 1024 tokens is not significantly better than \texttt{no\_trace} (75.13\%; $\Delta{=}{+}2.10$pp, $p{=}0.078$, 95\% CI [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]).
399	This non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation: the 1024-token prefix retains enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events that appear near the trace terminus.
400	Under tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}); only DeepSeek-6.7B shows a mild $-$1.5pp decrease at tail-1024$\to$tail-2048, attributable to architecture-specific long-context sensitivity rather than a systematic mid-length pathology.
401	
402	\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}
403	All four trace budget conditions significantly exceed \texttt{no\_trace} except 1024 tokens ($p{=}0.078$), confirming that raw trace content provides some signal but unreliably so.
404	The gap to \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) is massive for every budget condition (18--23pp, all $p{<}0.001$), underscoring that even unlimited raw traces cannot substitute for targeted signal extraction.
405	The best full-trace condition (no\_limit, 81.76\%) still lags \texttt{exception\_only} by 18.07pp (95\% CI [$-$20.34, $-$15.88]).
406	
407	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
408	These results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.
409	The non-monotonic dip at 1024 tokens is consistent with a ``worst-of-both-worlds'' regime where the model receives enough noisy context to dilute the signal but not enough to compensate through broader coverage.
410	The result reinforces the information density interpretation (\S\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \emph{length}.
411	In contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
412	
413	\paragraph{Truncation direction.}
414	Tail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: \texttt{tail\_2048} achieves 97.31\% vs.\ \texttt{head\_2048} (no\_limit) at 81.76\%, a 15.55pp improvement.
415	Even \texttt{tail\_512} (93.61\%) exceeds every head-truncation condition by over 11pp.
416	
417	However, \texttt{tail\_256} (77.48\%) performs at the \texttt{no\_trace} baseline level (75.13\%), indicating that the diagnostic signal does not reside in the very last trace lines.
418	The critical jump occurs between 256 and 512 tail tokens (+16.13pp), localizing the signal window to approximately 256--512 tokens from the trace terminus.
419	This is consistent with Python's execution model: exception tracebacks and assertion comparisons appear near the end of the trace but are preceded by the final function call frames, placing them typically 300--500 tokens from the terminus rather than at the absolute end.
420	
421	The 18.07pp gap from \texttt{full\_trace} to \texttt{exception\_only} decomposes as: 86\% positional concentration (recovered by tail-truncation at 2048 tokens) and 14\% residual content noise (the 2.52pp gap from \texttt{tail\_2048} to \texttt{exception\_only} that persists regardless of truncation direction).
422	The fact that \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) still outperforms \texttt{tail\_2048} (97.31\%) confirms that semantic extraction (identifying the exception type and message) is superior to positional extraction (keeping a fixed window), because the model need not filter out non-diagnostic lines that happen to fall within the window.
423	
424	\paragraph{Cross-model budget curve.}
425	Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} extends the tail-truncation analysis to a full budget sweep across four models spanning three architecture families and an 8.7$\times$ parameter range.
426	
427	\begin{table}[t]
428	\centering
429	\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$). 464	
465	Four patterns emerge from the budget curve.
466	\emph{Phase transition between 384 and 512 tokens}: the tail-384 column reveals that the transition is not instantaneous at 256$\to$512 but localized between 384 and 512 tokens.
467	At tail-384, all four models remain near baseline (73.6--82.9\%), gaining only +1.0--6.5pp over tail-256.
468	The steep jump occurs at 384$\to$512: +13.7pp (Qwen-7B), +11.0pp (DeepSeek), +17.6pp (CodeLlama), +22.0pp (Qwen-1.5B), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling.
469	This localizes the critical threshold to ${\sim}$400--500 tokens, consistent with the typical exception traceback length: below ${\sim}$384 tokens, the traceback is truncated and no diagnostic event is captured; above ${\sim}$512, it is fully included regardless of model capacity or architecture.
470	
471	\emph{Convergence at tail-512+}: once the phase transition is crossed, accuracy converges across all models into a 93.6--98.2\% band at tail-512 (seed 42).
472	Multi-seed validation confirms robustness: across all 12 model$\times$seed combinations at tail-512 (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}), all accuracies fall within [93.61\%, 98.24\%], a 4.63pp band that compresses the signal-poor tail-256 spread (6.3pp) by 27\%.
473	CodeLlama-13B shows perfect per-model convergence at tail-512: all three seeds achieve 98.24\% ($\sigma{=}$0), consistent with the 13B scale and label-only loss providing a stronger optimization signal in the signal-rich regime (Table~\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}).
474

478	\label{tab:tail512_seeds}

492	\end{tabular}%
493	}
494	\end{table}
495	
496	At tail-1024, four models reach 96.5--98.2\% (CodeLlama 98.2\% under label-only loss, three others 96.5--96.6\%).
497	Scale and architecture differences that dominate the signal-poor regime (tail-256 range: 72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp) compress dramatically in the signal-rich regime.
498	
499	\emph{Signal-poor convergence band}: head-2048 accuracies across all four models also converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\% band (6.5pp; Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}), comparable in width to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\%, 6.3pp).
500	When the diagnostic signal is absent (head-truncation discards trace terminus) or unreachable (tail-256 is too short), models of different scale and architecture collapse to similar performance, suggesting a shared floor determined by code comprehension rather than trace processing.
501	
502	\emph{Scale behavior inversion}: in the signal-poor regime (tail-256), smaller models perform worse (Qwen-1.5B 72.6\% vs.\ Qwen-7B 78.9\%, $-$6.3pp), mirroring the \texttt{no\_trace} scale pattern.
503	In the signal-rich regime (tail-512+), this reverses: Qwen-1.5B matches or exceeds Qwen-7B (95.6\% vs.\ 93.6\% at tail-512; 97.9\% vs.\ 97.3\% at tail-2048), confirming that sufficient trace signal eliminates the capacity advantage.
504	
505	\emph{Non-monotonicity at long sequences}: two models show accuracy decreases beyond tail-1024.
506	DeepSeek-6.7B drops $-$1.5pp from tail-1024 (96.5\%) to tail-2048 (95.0\%), consistent with its known long-context sensitivity (\S\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
507	CodeLlama-13B exhibits a more severe collapse: tail-2048 under label-only loss drops to 41.8\% (vs.\ tail-1024 = 98.2\%), which we attribute to LoRA long-sequence instability under the label-only loss regime; this outlier is excluded from cross-model tail-2048 analysis.
508	Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B increase monotonically across all budgets.
509	
510	Table~\ref{tab:head_vs_tail} confirms the truncation direction effect: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 outperforms head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp.
511	The gap is largest for Qwen-1.5B (+22.6pp), indicating that smaller models benefit most from positional proximity to the diagnostic signal, as they lack the capacity to locate it within longer, noisier prefixes.
512	
513	%% ============================================================
514	\section{Calibration Analysis}
515	\label{sec:calibration_full}
516	
517	Beyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \citep{spiess2025calibration}.
518	
519	\begin{table}[t]
520	\centering
521	\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}
522	\label{tab:calibration}
523	\small
524	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
525	\toprule
526	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{ECE $\downarrow$} & \textbf{Brier $\downarrow$} \\
527	\midrule
528	ae\_only & \textbf{0.0004} & \textbf{0.0008} \\
529	exception\_only & 0.0027 & 0.0013 \\
530	VOTA & 0.0054 & 0.005 \\
531	full\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\
532	no\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\
533	\bottomrule
534	\end{tabular}
535	\end{table}
536	
537	Table~\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.
538	Tier~1 conditions (\texttt{ae\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \texttt{exception\_only}: ECE=0.0027) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \texttt{full\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \texttt{no\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.
539	VOTA (ECE=0.0054) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.
540	
541	The Brier scores tell a consistent story: \texttt{ae\_only} (0.0008) and \texttt{exception\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \texttt{no\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.
542	
543	\paragraph{Cross-model calibration.}
544	Table~\ref{tab:calibration_cross} extends the calibration analysis to four models (1.5B--13B) under \texttt{exception\_only}.
545	All four models achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 and Brier ${<}$ 0.007, confirming that calibration quality generalizes across architectures and scales.
546	CodeLlama-13B achieves the lowest ECE (0.0020) and MCE (0.0411), while DeepSeek-6.7B shows the highest ECE (0.0064) and Brier (0.0061), consistent with its higher error rate (8 errors vs.\ 2 for Qwen/CodeLlama).

572	%% ============================================================
573	\section{Error Overlap Analysis}
574	\label{sec:error_overlap}
575	
576	We analyze the overlap between error sets of different conditions to determine whether they fail on the same samples (Figure~\ref{fig:error_overlap}).
577	
578	\texttt{exception\_only}'s 2 errors (samples 901 and 905) have zero intersection with the error sets of all other conditions (Jaccard = 0.000 in all pairwise comparisons).
579	This orthogonality arises because \texttt{exception\_only} errors are caused by \texttt{GeneratorExit} false positives (pass samples misclassified as fail due to benign \texttt{BaseException} events), while errors from other conditions are predominantly fail samples misclassified as pass due to insufficient signal.
580	
581	Among non-exception conditions, error sets exhibit hierarchical containment: \texttt{ae\_only} $\subset$ VOTA $\subset$ \texttt{loop\_only} $\subset$ \texttt{no\_trace}, with pairwise Jaccard indices increasing with signal overlap.
582	\texttt{loop\_only} and \texttt{full\_trace} share the highest overlap (Jaccard ${\sim}$0.65), reflecting their shared reliance on behavioral patterns rather than explicit fault indicators.
583	
584	This analysis confirms that \texttt{exception\_only} operates on an independent signal dimension: its errors are representation ambiguities (iterator protocol artifacts) rather than information deficits, explaining why combining it with other signals cannot eliminate these specific errors.
585	
586	\begin{figure}[t]
587	\centering
588	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{error_overlap}
589	\caption{Jaccard similarity between error sets of different conditions (Qwen-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$). \texttt{exception\_only} (exc\_only) has zero overlap with all other conditions, confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension.}
590	\label{fig:error_overlap}
591	\end{figure}

593	%% ============================================================
594	\section{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}
595	\label{sec:debugbench_full}
596	
597	To test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \citep{tian2024debugbench}, a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).
598	After filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\%).
599	
600	\begin{table}[t]
601	\centering
602	\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is preserved, and exception\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}
603	\label{tab:debugbench}
604	\small
605	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
606	\toprule
607	\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Acc (\%)} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
608	\midrule
609	exception\_only & \textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\
610	VOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\
611	no\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\
612	\midrule
613	Majority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\
614	\bottomrule
615	\end{tabular}
616	\end{table}
617	
618	Table~\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \texttt{exception\_only} (100.00\%) $\geq$ VOTA (99.77\%) $>$ \texttt{no\_trace} (96.71\%).
619	The \texttt{exception\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, confirming that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.
620	
621	The absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \texttt{no\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\% (compared to 75.13\% on HumanEval+MBPP), leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.
622	This ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.
623	In contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.
624	
625	These results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach: the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\_only $\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\_trace) is consistent across both datasets. However, the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.
626	The 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\_trace 75.13\% $\to$ exception\_only 99.83\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals.
627	
628	%% ============================================================
629	\section{Cross-Model Robustness Analysis}
630	\label{sec:robustness_analysis}
631	
632	The cross-model gap pattern (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}
633	Exception events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity (a fixed-format error message) ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.
634	Two of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\%) we hypothesize reflects LoRA initialization sensitivity landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\%, though direct evidence is lacking), not a failure of the exception signal itself.
635	VOTA achieves ${\geq}$98.57\% across all models when excluding outlier seeds (cross-model range: 1.01pp), but DeepSeek VOTA exhibits seed-dependent instability: seed 42 (84.45\%) is an outlier due to tokenizer-induced truncation (91.7\% of training samples affected), while seeds 123/456 (99.16\%/99.33\%) match cross-model performance.
636	This seed sensitivity is confounded by tokenizer efficiency and base model differences \citep{yang2024generalization}.
637	Our \texttt{exception\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely: inputs have a median of 150 tokens (mean 398), well below the 2{,}048-token context window, so no training samples are truncated on either model.
638	The resulting 0.56pp cross-model gap (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.
639	The practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal; for smaller models, training stability (multi-seed selection, warmup schedules) is a secondary but addressable concern.
640	
641	%% ============================================================
642	\section{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}
643	\label{sec:training_dynamics}
644	
645	The DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.
646	First, epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in 2/3 seeds: seed 42 peaks at 99.83\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), seed 123 degrades from 91.43\% to 91.09\% ($-$0.34pp), while seed 456 shows no epoch degradation (99.33\% at both epoch~1 and epoch~3).
647	Early stopping may yield marginal gains for some seeds, but the epoch effect is small (${\leq}$0.5pp) compared to the seed effect (8.24pp between seeds 42 and 123).
648	
649	Second, seeds 123 and 456 converge to nearly identical final training losses (0.122 and 0.123 respectively), yet their accuracies differ by 8.24pp (91.09\% vs.\ 99.33\%).
650	The critical difference lies in fail recall: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 100\%, while seed 123 achieves only 70.5\%. The LoRA initialization in seed 123 fails to learn the exception$\to$fail mapping for a substantial fraction of failing samples, even though the per-token loss appears converged.
651	This demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, paralleling the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 (where eval loss rose 65\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved from 78.15\% to 84.45\%).
652	The practical recommendation is twofold: (1)~use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training or evaluation loss, and (2)~for deployment on smaller models, train multiple seeds and select the best, as a single run may not reliably converge to the correct classification boundary.
653	
654	\paragraph{Format collapse, not semantic failure.}
655	The 100 errors of seed 123 are not semantic failures but generation format collapse: on inputs exceeding 4,000 characters (those containing verbose locals dumps), the model echoes trace content instead of emitting a verdict token, while achieving zero errors on shorter inputs.
656	This indicates that the LoRA initialization of seed 123 specifically compromises long-context format compliance rather than exception signal comprehension, further supporting the robustness advantage of compact \texttt{exception\_only} inputs.
657	
658	The contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\% to 84.45\% across epochs, consistent with a performance limit from high trace truncation (91.7\% of samples; \S\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).
659	Seeds 123/456, which avoid the truncation-sensitive initialization basin, achieve 99.16\%/99.33\%, confirming that the deficit is seed-specific rather than a fundamental model limitation.
660	
661	%% ============================================================
662	\section{Deduplication Statistics}
663	\label{sec:dedup_stats}
664	
665	Table~\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across trace formats.
666	Because multiple passing solutions to the same problem produce identical (input, output) pairs in the \texttt{exception\_only} format (correct executions raise no exceptions, so the trace content is the same regardless of the solution's internal logic), the dataset contains duplicate rows.
667	Train--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID), so no information leakage occurs.
668	The high duplication in \texttt{exception\_only} does not affect our experimental conclusions: after deduplication the training set still contains 2,858 unique samples and the test set 607, providing sufficient coverage for the 591-problem benchmark.
669	
670	\paragraph{Problem-level partitioning and sensitivity analysis.}
671	Data is partitioned at the problem level: 472 training problems and 119 test problems, with zero crossover.
672	Duplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem (up to 10 solutions each), not from cross-problem overlap.
673	To verify that deduplication does not alter any finding, we ran a sensitivity analysis across all 19 experimental conditions (7 trace formats $\times$ 4 models, minus conditions not run): deduplication produces a 0.00pp accuracy delta in every case.
674	This confirms that duplicate samples neither inflate nor deflate accuracy, because duplicates share the same ground-truth label and the model's prediction is deterministic at inference time.
675	The \texttt{exception\_only} test set contains 48.99\% duplicate inputs due to representation compactness; all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.
676	
677	\begin{table}[t]
678	\centering
679	\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \texttt{exception\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}
680	\label{tab:dedup}

683	\toprule
684	\textbf{Split} & \textbf{Total} & \textbf{Unique} & \textbf{Dup.\ rate} \\
```
